Considering that prime powers become less frequent as numbers get larger, and given that the expression $5b^3 +6b^2 +5b +4$ grows as $5b^3$, the values for larger $b$ will be astronomically large, making it impractical to check each one. However, perhaps there are no other solutions. But the problem states "For which bases $b>6$", so we need to check if base7 is the only one. But maybe there\'s a base higher than 50 where this expression is a prime power. But it\'s unclear. Alternatively, perhaps we need to think of other approaches.\n\nAlternatively, instead of testing bases sequentially, perhaps check if for some prime powers $p^k$, solve for $b$ in the equation $5b^3 +6b^2 +5b +4 =p^k$. However, solving for $b$ is non-trivial.\n\nAlternatively, factor the polynomial $5b^3 +6b^2 +5b +4$. Let\'s attempt to factor it.\n\nLet’s check if the polynomial can be factored:\n\n$5b^3 +6b^2 +5b +4$.\n\nPossible factors could be of the form $(ab + c)(5b^2 + db + e)$. Let\'s attempt to factor it.\n\nAlternatively, use rational root theorem. Possible rational roots are factors of 4 (constant term) over factors of 5 (leading coefficient). So possible roots: ±1, ±2, ±4, ±1/5, ±2/5, ±4/5.\n\nTesting these in the polynomial:\n\nAt b=1: 5+6+5+4=10 ≠0\n\nAt b=2: 40 +24 +10 +4=78 ≠0\n\nAt b=3: 135 + 162 +15 +4=316≠0\n\nAt b=4: 320 + 96 +20 +4=436≠0\n\nAt b= -1: -5 +6 -5 +4=0 So, (b +1) is a factor.\n\nTherefore, factor as (5b2 + some terms)(b +1). Then perform polynomial division or use synthesis division.\n\nDivide $5b^3 +6b^2 +5b +4$ by $(b +1)$.\n\nUsing synthetic division:\n\nCoefficient array: 5,6,5,4 | -1\n\n      5* (-1) = -5\n\n       6 + (-5)=1\n\n1 + 5*(-1)= -4\n\n-4 + 4 =0\n\nThe remainder is 0. Thus, the polynomial factors as (b +1)(5b2 +1).\n\nThus, $5b^3 +6b^2 +5b +4=(b +1)(5b2 +1)$.\n\nTherefore, for the original expression to be a prime power, either $(b +1)$ or $(5b2 +1)$ must be a prime power. Since $b$ is a base greater than 6, $b +1$ is a number between 7 and 8. The only prime power in that range is 7. Therefore, if $b +1=7$, then $b=6$, which is not greater than 6. Therefore, discard $b +1=7$ (so $b=6$), but since $b>6$, we need to check if $5b2 +1$ is a prime power.\n\nThus, $5b2 +1 = p^k$ where $p$ is prime. So solve for $b >6: 5b2 +1 = p^k$.\n\nTherefore, the problem reduces to finding bases $b>6$ such that $5b2 +1$ is a prime power.\n\nTherefore, by factoring the original polynomial, we can narrow down the problem to solving $5b2 +1 = p^k$.\n\nTherefore, let\'s set $5b2 +1 = p^k$, with $b >6$ and $p$ prime, $k \\geq 1$.\n\nWe need to find all integers $b >6$ such that $5b2 +1$ is a prime power.\n\nThis is easier to manage than the original problem.\n\nSo now, our task is to find all integers $b>6$ where $5b2 +1$ is a prime power. Since $5b2 +1$ must be a prime power, let\'s consider that $5b2 +1 = p^k$, where $p$ is prime and $k \\geq 1$.\n\nFirst, note that if $p=2$, check whether $5b2 +1$ is a power of 2.\n\nIf $5b2 +1$ is even, then $5b2$ must be odd, which would mean that $5b2$ is odd, so $b2$ is odd, implying $b$ is odd. Then $5b2$ is congruent to 5*(1) mod 2=1 mod 2, so $5b2 +1≡0 mod 2$, so if $5b2 +1$ is a power of 2 and even, it must be at least 4. Therefore, $5b2 +1=2^m$, where $m≥2$.\n\nSo if $5b2 +1$ is a power of 2, subtract 1: $5b2=2^m -1$. So, $5b2=2^m -1$. Then $b2=(2^m -1)/5$. Therefore, $(2^m -1)$ must be divisible by 5.\n\nCheck when $2^m ≡1 mod5$. The multiplicative order of 2 mod5 is 4, since 2^1=2, 2^2=4, 2^3=3, 2^4=1 mod5. Thus, $2^m ≡1 mod5$ when m divisible by4. Therefore, m=4,8,12,...\n\nTherefore, for m=4: $2^4 -1=16-1=15$, so $b2=15/5=3→ b=√3$, not integer.\n\nm=8: $2^8-1=256-1=255$, so $b2=255/5=51→b=√51≈7.14$, not integer.\n\nm=12: $2^{12}-1=4096-1=4095$, $4095/5=819$. $b2=819→b=√819≈28.62$, not integer.\n\nm=16: $2^{16}-1=65536-1=65535$, $65535/5=13107$. $√13107≈114.9$, and so on. These b are not integers, so no solution with p=2.\n\nTherefore, there are no solutions where $5b2 +1$ is a power of 2 with b>6.\n\nNext, check if $5b2 +1$ is an odd prime power.
